Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today. I really hope I'm not taking up too much of your time with this... it's a little silly, really. I'm usually perfectly fine, you know, very independent. 

But... well, I suppose what finally made me come in is this little problem I've been having with driving. It's a bit embarrassing to admit at my age, but the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges heading toward Miami, I just have this awful reaction. My chest tightens right up, and my hands just clamp onto the steering wheel. I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I just have to get off the bridge right that very second, even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. 

I know it sounds ridiculous for an 86-year-old woman to get in such a state over a bridge. Is that... does that make sense to you? I hope I'm explaining it alright.

Well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it completely avoiding them, exactly. But, well, I have found myself making a few little adjustments lately. 

I've always been so independent, you know? I was always the one organizing the outings and driving everyone around. But lately, I've... well, I've been saying no to a few things. I even had to ask my daughter if she wouldn't mind driving me when I go to visit her, which... oh, it's just so embarrassing to admit. I hate being a bother to anyone. 

I just... I'd rather miss out than put myself through that silly feeling on the bridge if I have to drive it myself. I hope you don't think less of me for that, Doctor. It's just a little phase, I'm sure. Am I making a bigger deal out of this than I should be?

It's a bit embarrassing to admit at my age, but the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges heading toward Miami, I just have this awful reaction. My chest tightens right up, and my hands just clamp onto the steering wheel. I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I just have to get off the bridge right that very second, even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. \n\nI know it sounds ridiculous for an 86-year-old woman to get in such a state over a bridge. Is that... does that make sense to you? I hope I'm explaining it alright.\n\nDoctor: Does this overwhelming feeling happen to you in any other situations besides driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. It’s really just the bridges. I can drive to the grocery store, or over to visit my friends, and I’m perfectly fine. Not a care in the world. \n\nIt’s really just... well, those long ones over the water. I’m quite sensible the rest of the time, I promise. I hope that's a good sign? That it's just this one silly thing and not happening everywhere else. I really try not to let things bother me. Does that give you the information you need, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to die or lose control?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, nothing quite that dramatic. I mean, it's very unpleasant, of course—my heart flutters a bit and I feel a little flustered—but I certainly don't think I'm going to die or anything awful like that. I know I'm perfectly safe in my car. \n\nIt's just a little... a little wave of panic about being stuck up there, I suppose. But I always keep my wits about me. I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing my head out there on the road. I'm a very safe driver, truly. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving on bridges because of these panic feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it completely avoiding them, exactly. But, well, I have found myself making a few little adjustments lately. \n\nI've always been so independent, you know? I was always the one organizing the outings and driving everyone around. But lately, I've... well, I've been saying no to a few things. Like the gallery openings downtown, or those lovely birdwatching trips I used to enjoy so much. I even had to ask my daughter if she wouldn't mind driving me when I go to visit her, which... oh, it's just so embarrassing to admit. I hate being a bother to anyone. \n\nI just... I'd rather miss out than put myself through that silly feeling on the bridge if I have to drive it myself. I hope you don't think less of me for that, Doctor. It's just a little phase, I'm sure. Am I making a bigger deal out of this than I should be?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Well, let me think... I suppose it's been about 11 months now. Yes, right around then. 

You see, my daughter developed a bit of a heart condition—nothing she can't handle, she's very strong—but it does mean she needs to see a specialist down in Miami quite regularly. And, well, that drive involves some rather large bridges, as I'm sure you know.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose it's not so much the height, exactly... though they are quite tall, aren't they? It's more... well, it's the water. All that open water just stretching out everywhere. 

I mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and goodness knows plenty of people cross them every single day without a second thought. My daughter certainly does. And I know it's perfectly safe, logically speaking. I really do. But when I just think about driving over all that water... well, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. 

It sounds so silly when I say it out loud. I know I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, and I really don't want you to think I'm losing my wits or anything. It's just a strange little quirk, I suppose. Does that make any sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it properly.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well... I certainly try to. I really do. Sometimes I sit quietly in my garden and close my eyes, and I tell myself, 'It's just a road, just a simple road.' And I want so badly to just picture myself driving across it with a smile. 

But... well, the moment I try to imagine actually being up there, with all that water around, my chest does this silly little flutter. I suppose I haven't quite been able to picture it being completely calm, as much as I wish I could. I know I should be able to—a woman my age ought to have more sense than this. I'm trying very hard to be positive about it, though. 

I hope you don't think I'm just being difficult or stubborn? I really do want to get past this so I can be more help to my daughter. I just haven't quite gotten the hang of imagining it yet, I suppose. Is it alright that I'm having a little trouble with that part?

My chest tightens right up, and my hands just clamp onto the steering wheel. I hope I'm explaining it alright.\n\nDoctor: Does this overwhelming feeling happen to you in any other situations besides driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. It’s really just the bridges. I can drive to the grocery store, or over to visit my friends, and I’m perfectly fine. Not a care in the world. \n\nIt’s really just... well, those long ones over the water. I’m quite sensible the rest of the time, I promise. I hope that's a good sign? Does that give you the information you need, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to die or lose control?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, nothing quite that dramatic. I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing my head out there on the road. I'm a very safe driver, truly. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving on bridges because of these panic feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it completely avoiding them, exactly. But, well, I have found myself making a few little adjustments lately. \n\nI've always been so independent, you know? I was always the one organizing the outings and driving everyone around. But lately, I've... well, I've been saying no to a few things. Like the gallery openings downtown, or those lovely birdwatching trips I used to enjoy so much. I even had to ask my daughter if she wouldn't mind driving me when I go to visit her, which... oh, it's just so embarrassing to admit. I hate being a bother to anyone. \n\nI just... I'd rather miss out than put myself through that silly feeling on the bridge if I have to drive it myself. I hope you don't think less of me for that, Doctor. It's just a little phase, I'm sure. I'm perfectly content. \n\nI've always believed in looking on the bright side, you know? There's no sense in borrowing trouble. I really do try my best to be helpful to her.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that bothers you, or is it something else about them?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's not so much the height, exactly... though they are quite tall, aren't they? All that open water just stretching out everywhere. \n\nI mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and goodness knows plenty of people cross them every single day without a second thought. My daughter certainly does. And I know it's perfectly safe, logically speaking. I really do. But when I just think about driving over all that water... well, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's mostly when I'm the one driving, but... well, to be perfectly honest, I've started avoiding them altogether, even as a passenger. 

I feel so foolish admitting this, but I've actually started turning down invitations to things I used to love. Little gallery openings, my birdwatching group trips... even some visits with my daughter, unless someone else can drive me over those bridges. And even then, I just... I'd rather not go if I can help it. 

It's terribly embarrassing, really. I've always been the one who planned everything and handled things myself, you know? I've always prided myself on being independent. And now I'm making a fuss and missing out on things just because of a silly road. I hope you don't think less of me for it... I know it's not very becoming of someone my age to act this way. I really am trying to be better about it, I promise. Does that make sense?

I suppose I haven't quite been able to picture it being completely calm, as much as I wish I could. I know I should be able to—a woman my age ought to have more sense than this. I'm trying very hard to be positive about it, though. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm just being difficult or stubborn? I really do want to get past this so I can be more help to my daughter. I just haven't quite gotten the hang of imagining it yet, I suppose. Is it alright that I'm having a little trouble with that part?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this anxiety when you're a passenger in a car driving on a bridge, or is it only when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly when I'm the one driving, but... well, to be perfectly honest, I've started avoiding them altogether, even as a passenger. \n\nI feel so foolish admitting this, but I've actually started turning down invitations to things I used to love. Little gallery openings, my birdwatching group trips... even some visits with my daughter, unless someone else can drive me over those bridges. And even then, I just... I'd rather not go if I can help it. \n\nIt's terribly embarrassing, really. I've always been the one who planned everything and handled things myself, you know? I've always prided myself on being independent. And now I'm making a fuss and missing out on things just because of a silly road. I hope you don't think less of me for it... I know it's not very becoming of someone my age to act this way. I really am trying to be better about it, I promise. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel anxious when you are on a bridge but not in a car, such as when walking or biking?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 86 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, walking or biking? Well, I... I haven't ridden a bicycle in years, I'm afraid. And as for walking... well, I really couldn't say. 

You see, the bridges around here are mostly for the highway, so there aren't really pedestrian paths on them anyway. I haven't actually had the occasion to try walking across one. I suppose if I had to, I'd try my very best to be brave about it! But since it hasn't come up, I just... I don't really know how I'd feel. 

I hope that's an alright answer? I'm sorry I can't be more helpful with that one. I always try to give you the best information I can, Doctor. I hope I'm doing okay?

Oh, well... no, I don't think so. Just thinking about them doesn't really bother me. I mean, I know they're just structures, just concrete and steel, after all. 

When I'm sitting in my living room, having my tea, I can think about a bridge perfectly fine. It's really only when I'm faced with the prospect of actually getting in a car and going over one that things get a bit... uncomfortable. But just the thought of them? No, that's perfectly alright. 

I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm trying to be as clear as possible, I really am.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today. I really hope I'm not taking up too much of your time with this... it's a little silly, really. I'm usually perfectly fine, you know, very independent. \n\nBut... well, I suppose what finally made me come in is this little problem I've been having with driving. It's a bit embarrassing to admit at my age, but the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges heading toward Miami, I just have this awful reaction. My chest tightens right up, and my hands just clamp onto the steering wheel. I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I just have to get off the bridge right that very second, even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. \n\nI know it sounds ridiculous for an 86-year-old woman to get in such a state over a bridge. Is that... does that make sense to you? I hope I'm explaining it alright.\n\nDoctor: Does this overwhelming feeling happen to you in any other situations besides driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. It’s really just the bridges. I can drive to the grocery store, or over to visit my friends, and I’m perfectly fine. Not a care in the world. \n\nIt’s really just... well, those long ones over the water. I’m quite sensible the rest of the time, I promise. I hope that's a good sign? That it's just this one silly thing and not happening everywhere else. I really try not to let things bother me. Does that give you the information you need, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to die or lose control?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, nothing quite that dramatic. I mean, it's very unpleasant, of course—my heart flutters a bit and I feel a little flustered—but I certainly don't think I'm going to die or anything awful like that. I know I'm perfectly safe in my car. \n\nIt's just a little... a little wave of panic about being stuck up there, I suppose. But I always keep my wits about me. I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing my head out there on the road. I'm a very safe driver, truly. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving on bridges because of these panic feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it completely avoiding them, exactly. But, well, I have found myself making a few little adjustments lately. \n\nI've always been so independent, you know? I was always the one organizing the outings and driving everyone around. But lately, I've... well, I've been saying no to a few things. Like the gallery openings downtown, or those lovely birdwatching trips I used to enjoy so much. I even had to ask my daughter if she wouldn't mind driving me when I go to visit her, which... oh, it's just so embarrassing to admit. I hate being a bother to anyone. \n\nI just... I'd rather miss out than put myself through that silly feeling on the bridge if I have to drive it myself. I hope you don't think less of me for that, Doctor. It's just a little phase, I'm sure. Am I making a bigger deal out of this than I should be?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety or worry about things other than driving on bridges in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all. I'm really quite a cheerful person, usually. I don't sit around worrying about things, goodness no. I have a lovely garden to tend to, and my books... I'm perfectly content. \n\nI've always believed in looking on the bright side, you know? There's no sense in borrowing trouble. It really is just this... this one silly little bridge thing that's been bothering me. The rest of my life is very peaceful, I assure you. I'm not a nervous sort of person in general. I hope that helps clarify things? I wouldn't want you to think I'm just a bundle of nerves about everything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this anxiety when driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I suppose it's been about 11 months now. Yes, right around then. \n\nYou see, my daughter developed a bit of a heart condition—nothing she can't handle, she's very strong—but it does mean she needs to see a specialist down in Miami quite regularly. And, well, that drive involves some rather large bridges, as I'm sure you know. \n\nBefore that, I didn't have much reason to go that way, so it really wasn't an issue. I hope it doesn't sound like I'm making excuses... I know I should be able to just drive her without making a fuss. It's just that the trips started becoming a bit frequent, and that's when I noticed this... this little hesitation. I'm sure it's just a temporary thing, don't you think? I really do try my best to be helpful to her.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that bothers you, or is it something else about them?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's not so much the height, exactly... though they are quite tall, aren't they? It's more... well, it's the water. All that open water just stretching out everywhere. \n\nI mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and goodness knows plenty of people cross them every single day without a second thought. My daughter certainly does. And I know it's perfectly safe, logically speaking. I really do. But when I just think about driving over all that water... well, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud. I know I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, and I really don't want you to think I'm losing my wits or anything. It's just a strange little quirk, I suppose. Does that make any sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it properly.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to imagine yourself driving on a bridge without feeling any anxiety?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I certainly try to. I really do. Sometimes I sit quietly in my garden and close my eyes, and I tell myself, 'It's just a road, just a simple road.' And I want so badly to just picture myself driving across it with a smile. \n\nBut... well, the moment I try to imagine actually being up there, with all that water around, my chest does this silly little flutter. I suppose I haven't quite been able to picture it being completely calm, as much as I wish I could. I know I should be able to—a woman my age ought to have more sense than this. I'm trying very hard to be positive about it, though. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm just being difficult or stubborn? I really do want to get past this so I can be more help to my daughter. Oh, well... if someone else is driving? I suppose... well, yes, I still feel a bit anxious, but I can manage it if I absolutely must. I'd much rather avoid it, though. 

Actually, I... well, I haven't been going out quite as much lately because of it. I've started turning down invitations to gallery openings, and... and my birdwatching trips, which I usually adore. I've even missed a few visits with my daughter recently, unless she or someone else can come and drive me over those bridges. 

It's... well, it's terribly embarrassing to admit, if I'm being honest. I've always been the one who planned everything, the one who handled things myself. I've always been so independent, you see. To have to rely on others just to get across a bridge... it makes me feel a bit silly. 

I do hope you don't think less of me for it. I really am trying my best to handle it gracefully. Is it... is it normal to feel so embarrassed about something like this, Doctor?

But when I just think about driving over all that water... well, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud. I know I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, and I really don't want you to think I'm losing my wits or anything. It's just a strange little quirk, I suppose. I certainly try to. I really do. Sometimes I sit quietly in my garden and close my eyes, and I tell myself, 'It's just a road, just a simple road.' I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving on bridges because of these panic feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it completely avoiding them, exactly. But, well, I have found myself making a few little adjustments lately. \n\nI've always been so independent, you know? I was always the one organizing the outings and driving everyone around. But lately, I've... well, I've been saying no to a few things. Like the gallery openings downtown, or those lovely birdwatching trips I used to enjoy so much. I even had to ask my daughter if she wouldn't mind driving me when I go to visit her, which... oh, it's just so embarrassing to admit. I hate being a bother to anyone. \n\nI just... I'd rather miss out than put myself through that silly feeling on the bridge if I have to drive it myself. I hope you don't think less of me for that, Doctor. It's just a little phase, I'm sure. Am I making a bigger deal out of this than I should be?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety or worry about things other than driving on bridges in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all. I'm really quite a cheerful person, usually. I don't sit around worrying about things, goodness no. I have a lovely garden to tend to, and my books... I'm perfectly content. \n\nI've always believed in looking on the bright side, you know? There's no sense in borrowing trouble. It really is just this... this one silly little bridge thing that's been bothering me. The rest of my life is very peaceful, I assure you. I'm not a nervous sort of person in general. I hope that helps clarify things? I wouldn't want you to think I'm just a bundle of nerves about everything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this anxiety when driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I suppose it's been about 11 months now. Yes, right around then. \n\nYou see, my daughter developed a bit of a heart condition—nothing she can't handle, she's very strong—but it does mean she needs to see a specialist down in Miami quite regularly. And, well, that drive involves some rather large bridges, as I'm sure you know. \n\nBefore that, I didn't have much reason to go that way, so it really wasn't an issue. I hope it doesn't sound like I'm making excuses... I know I should be able to just drive her without making a fuss. It's just that the trips started becoming a bit frequent, and that's when I noticed this... this little hesitation. I'm sure it's just a temporary thing, don't you think? I really do try my best to be helpful to her.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that bothers you, or is it something else about them?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's not so much the height, exactly... though they are quite tall, aren't they? It's more... well, it's the water. All that open water just stretching out everywhere. \n\nI mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and goodness knows plenty of people cross them every single day without a second thought. My daughter certainly does. And I know it's perfectly safe, logically speaking. I really do. But when I just think about driving over all that water... well, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud. I know I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, and I really don't want you to think I'm losing my wits or anything. It's just a strange little quirk, I suppose. Does that make any sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it properly.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to imagine yourself driving on a bridge without feeling any anxiety?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I certainly try to. I really do. Sometimes I sit quietly in my garden and close my eyes, and I tell myself, 'It's just a road, just a simple road.' And I want so badly to just picture myself driving across it with a smile. \n\nBut... well, the moment I try to imagine actually being up there, with all that water around, my chest does this silly little flutter. I suppose I haven't quite been able to picture it being completely calm, as much as I wish I could. I know I should be able to—a woman my age ought to have more sense than this. I'm trying very hard to be positive about it, though. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm just being difficult or stubborn? I really do want to get past this so I can be more help to my daughter. I just haven't quite gotten the hang of imagining it yet, I suppose. Is it alright that I'm having a little trouble with that part?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this anxiety when you're a passenger in a car driving on a bridge, or is it only when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly when I'm the one driving, but... well, to be perfectly honest, I've started avoiding them altogether, even as a passenger. \n\nI feel so foolish admitting this, but I've actually started turning down invitations to things I used to love. Little gallery openings, my birdwatching group trips... even some visits with my daughter, unless someone else can drive me over those bridges. And even then, I just... I'd rather not go if I can help it. \n\nIt's terribly embarrassing, really. I've always been the one who planned everything and handled things myself, you know? I've always prided myself on being independent. And now I'm making a fuss and missing out on things just because of a silly road. I hope you don't think less of me for it... I know it's not very becoming of someone my age to act this way. I really am trying to be better about it, I promise. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are on a bridge but not in a car, such as when walking or biking?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, walking or biking? Well, I... I haven't ridden a bicycle in years, I'm afraid. And as for walking... well, I really couldn't say. \n\nYou see, the bridges around here are mostly for the highway, so there aren't really pedestrian paths on them anyway. I haven't actually had the occasion to try walking across one. I suppose if I had to, I'd try my very best to be brave about it! But since it hasn't come up, I just... I don't really know how I'd feel. \n\nI hope that's an alright answer? I'm sorry I can't be more helpful with that one. I always try to give you the best information I can, Doctor. I hope I'm doing okay?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if just thinking about being on a bridge, without actually being on one or driving, makes you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I don't think so. Just thinking about them doesn't really bother me. I mean, I know they're just structures, just concrete and steel, after all. \n\nWhen I'm sitting in my living room, having my tea, I can think about a bridge perfectly fine. It's really only when I'm faced with the prospect of actually getting in a car and going over one that things get a bit... uncomfortable. But just the thought of them? No, that's perfectly alright. \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm trying to be as clear as possible, I really am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... nothing quite so dramatic as all that! I mean, when I'm right there at the edge of a bridge, maybe my heart flutters just a tiny bit, but... but certainly not sweating or losing my breath or anything like that. \n\nI wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart! I've always been quite healthy and put-together, you know. So, no, I haven't had anything like that sudden, intense feeling you're describing. I just get a little... flustered about the driving part, that's all. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I really do try to keep a level head about me, mostly. I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information... am I doing alright with these answers, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if someone else was driving you across the bridge?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... if someone else is driving? I suppose... well, yes, I still feel a bit anxious, but I can manage it if I absolutely must. I'd much rather avoid it, though. \n\nActually, I... well, I haven't been going out quite as much lately because of it. I've started turning down invitations to gallery openings, and... and my birdwatching trips, which I usually adore. I've even missed a few visits with my daughter recently, unless she or someone else can come and drive me over those bridges. \n\nIt's... well, it's terribly embarrassing to admit, if I'm being honest. I've always been the one who planned everything, the one who handled things myself. I've always been so independent, you see. To have to rely on others just to get across a bridge... it makes me feel a bit silly. \n\nI do hope you don't think less of me for it. I really am trying my best to handle it gracefully. Is it... is it normal to feel so embarrassed about something like this, Doctor?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Oh, well... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. If someone else is driving, I do still feel a little... unsettled, I guess you could say. But it's certainly much more manageable than if I'm the one behind the wheel. 

When I'm driving, it's just... well, it feels like all the responsibility is on me, and that makes it quite a bit harder. But if my daughter is driving, I can usually just close my eyes and take a deep breath, and we get through it. It's not pleasant, but I can do it. 

I hope that makes sense? I know I must sound awfully fussy, making such distinctions. I really do try not to make a fuss when others are driving me. I just sit quietly until we're across. Is that... is that a helpful way to explain it, Doctor?

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, not at all! Tall buildings and balconies are perfectly fine. I actually have a lovely little balcony at my apartment, on the fourth floor, where I keep my potted geraniums. I go out there every morning to water them and enjoy the breeze. 

And, oh, I used to love going to the top of those observation decks in the city when my husband was alive... the views were just magnificent. So, no, heights themselves don't bother me in the slightest. 

It really is just... just the bridges, and specifically when I'm driving over them. I know it seems a bit particular, doesn't it? I hope I'm not making things too complicated for you to figure out. I do try to be as straightforward as I can... is this the sort of detail you need, Doctor?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today. I really hope I'm not taking up too much of your time with this... it's a little silly, really. I'm usually perfectly fine, you know, very independent. \n\nBut... well, I suppose what finally made me come in is this little problem I've been having with driving. It's a bit embarrassing to admit at my age, but the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges heading toward Miami, I just have this awful reaction. My chest tightens right up, and my hands just clamp onto the steering wheel. I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I just have to get off the bridge right that very second, even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. \n\nI know it sounds ridiculous for an 86-year-old woman to get in such a state over a bridge. Is that... does that make sense to you? I hope I'm explaining it alright.\n\nDoctor: Does this overwhelming feeling happen to you in any other situations besides driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. It’s really just the bridges. I can drive to the grocery store, or over to visit my friends, and I’m perfectly fine. Not a care in the world. \n\nIt’s really just... well, those long ones over the water. I’m quite sensible the rest of the time, I promise. I hope that's a good sign? That it's just this one silly thing and not happening everywhere else. I really try not to let things bother me. Does that give you the information you need, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to die or lose control?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, nothing quite that dramatic. I mean, it's very unpleasant, of course—my heart flutters a bit and I feel a little flustered—but I certainly don't think I'm going to die or anything awful like that. I know I'm perfectly safe in my car. \n\nIt's just a little... a little wave of panic about being stuck up there, I suppose. But I always keep my wits about me. I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing my head out there on the road. I'm a very safe driver, truly. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving on bridges because of these panic feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it completely avoiding them, exactly. But, well, I have found myself making a few little adjustments lately. \n\nI've always been so independent, you know? I was always the one organizing the outings and driving everyone around. But lately, I've... well, I've been saying no to a few things. Like the gallery openings downtown, or those lovely birdwatching trips I used to enjoy so much. I even had to ask my daughter if she wouldn't mind driving me when I go to visit her, which... oh, it's just so embarrassing to admit. I hate being a bother to anyone. \n\nI just... I'd rather miss out than put myself through that silly feeling on the bridge if I have to drive it myself. I hope you don't think less of me for that, Doctor. It's just a little phase, I'm sure. Am I making a bigger deal out of this than I should be?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety or worry about things other than driving on bridges in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all. I'm really quite a cheerful person, usually. I don't sit around worrying about things, goodness no. I have a lovely garden to tend to, and my books... I'm perfectly content. \n\nI've always believed in looking on the bright side, you know? There's no sense in borrowing trouble. It really is just this... this one silly little bridge thing that's been bothering me. The rest of my life is very peaceful, I assure you. I'm not a nervous sort of person in general. I hope that helps clarify things? I wouldn't want you to think I'm just a bundle of nerves about everything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this anxiety when driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I suppose it's been about 11 months now. Yes, right around then. \n\nYou see, my daughter developed a bit of a heart condition—nothing she can't handle, she's very strong—but it does mean she needs to see a specialist down in Miami quite regularly. And, well, that drive involves some rather large bridges, as I'm sure you know. \n\nBefore that, I didn't have much reason to go that way, so it really wasn't an issue. I hope it doesn't sound like I'm making excuses... I know I should be able to just drive her without making a fuss. It's just that the trips started becoming a bit frequent, and that's when I noticed this... this little hesitation. I'm sure it's just a temporary thing, don't you think? I really do try my best to be helpful to her.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that bothers you, or is it something else about them?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's not so much the height, exactly... though they are quite tall, aren't they? It's more... well, it's the water. All that open water just stretching out everywhere. \n\nI mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and goodness knows plenty of people cross them every single day without a second thought. My daughter certainly does. And I know it's perfectly safe, logically speaking. I really do. But when I just think about driving over all that water... well, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud. I know I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, and I really don't want you to think I'm losing my wits or anything. It's just a strange little quirk, I suppose. Does that make any sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it properly.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to imagine yourself driving on a bridge without feeling any anxiety?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I certainly try to. I really do. Sometimes I sit quietly in my garden and close my eyes, and I tell myself, 'It's just a road, just a simple road.' And I want so badly to just picture myself driving across it with a smile. \n\nBut... well, the moment I try to imagine actually being up there, with all that water around, my chest does this silly little flutter. I suppose I haven't quite been able to picture it being completely calm, as much as I wish I could. I know I should be able to—a woman my age ought to have more sense than this. I'm trying very hard to be positive about it, though. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm just being difficult or stubborn? I really do want to get past this so I can be more help to my daughter. I just haven't quite gotten the hang of imagining it yet, I suppose. Is it alright that I'm having a little trouble with that part?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this anxiety when you're a passenger in a car driving on a bridge, or is it only when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly when I'm the one driving, but... well, to be perfectly honest, I've started avoiding them altogether, even as a passenger. \n\nI feel so foolish admitting this, but I've actually started turning down invitations to things I used to love. Little gallery openings, my birdwatching group trips... even some visits with my daughter, unless someone else can drive me over those bridges. And even then, I just... I'd rather not go if I can help it. \n\nIt's terribly embarrassing, really. I've always been the one who planned everything and handled things myself, you know? I've always prided myself on being independent. And now I'm making a fuss and missing out on things just because of a silly road. I hope you don't think less of me for it... I know it's not very becoming of someone my age to act this way. I really am trying to be better about it, I promise. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are on a bridge but not in a car, such as when walking or biking?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, walking or biking? Well, I... I haven't ridden a bicycle in years, I'm afraid. And as for walking... well, I really couldn't say. \n\nYou see, the bridges around here are mostly for the highway, so there aren't really pedestrian paths on them anyway. I haven't actually had the occasion to try walking across one. I suppose if I had to, I'd try my very best to be brave about it! But since it hasn't come up, I just... I don't really know how I'd feel. \n\nI hope that's an alright answer? I'm sorry I can't be more helpful with that one. I always try to give you the best information I can, Doctor. I hope I'm doing okay?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if just thinking about being on a bridge, without actually being on one or driving, makes you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I don't think so. Just thinking about them doesn't really bother me. I mean, I know they're just structures, just concrete and steel, after all. \n\nWhen I'm sitting in my living room, having my tea, I can think about a bridge perfectly fine. It's really only when I'm faced with the prospect of actually getting in a car and going over one that things get a bit... uncomfortable. But just the thought of them? No, that's perfectly alright. \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm trying to be as clear as possible, I really am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... nothing quite so dramatic as all that! I mean, when I'm right there at the edge of a bridge, maybe my heart flutters just a tiny bit, but... but certainly not sweating or losing my breath or anything like that. \n\nI wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart! I've always been quite healthy and put-together, you know. So, no, I haven't had anything like that sudden, intense feeling you're describing. I just get a little... flustered about the driving part, that's all. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I really do try to keep a level head about me, mostly. I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information... am I doing alright with these answers, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if someone else was driving you across the bridge?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... if someone else is driving? I suppose... well, yes, I still feel a bit anxious, but I can manage it if I absolutely must. I'd much rather avoid it, though. \n\nActually, I... well, I haven't been going out quite as much lately because of it. I've started turning down invitations to gallery openings, and... and my birdwatching trips, which I usually adore. I've even missed a few visits with my daughter recently, unless she or someone else can come and drive me over those bridges. \n\nIt's... well, it's terribly embarrassing to admit, if I'm being honest. I've always been the one who planned everything, the one who handled things myself. I've always been so independent, you see. To have to rely on others just to get across a bridge... it makes me feel a bit silly. \n\nI do hope you don't think less of me for it. I really am trying my best to handle it gracefully. Is it... is it normal to feel so embarrassed about something like this, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are on a bridge in a car with someone else driving, or is your anxiety only present when you are the one driving?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. If someone else is driving, I do still feel a little... unsettled, I guess you could say. But it's certainly much more manageable than if I'm the one behind the wheel. \n\nWhen I'm driving, it's just... well, it feels like all the responsibility is on me, and that makes it quite a bit harder. But if my daughter is driving, I can usually just close my eyes and take a deep breath, and we get through it. It's not pleasant, but I can do it. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I know I must sound awfully fussy, making such distinctions. I really do try not to make a fuss when others are driving me. I just sit quietly until we're across. Not a care in the world. \n\nIt’s really just... well, those long ones over the water. I’m quite sensible the rest of the time, I promise. I hope that's a good sign? That it's just this one silly thing and not happening everywhere else. I really try not to let things bother me. Does that give you the information you need, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to die or lose control?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, nothing quite that dramatic. I mean, it's very unpleasant, of course—my heart flutters a bit and I feel a little flustered—but I certainly don't think I'm going to die or anything awful like that. I know I'm perfectly safe in my car. \n\nIt's just a little... a little wave of panic about being stuck up there, I suppose. But I always keep my wits about me. I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing my head out there on the road. I'm a very safe driver, truly. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving on bridges because of these panic feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it completely avoiding them, exactly. But, well, I have found myself making a few little adjustments lately. \n\nI've always been so independent, you know? I was always the one organizing the outings and driving everyone around. But lately, I've... well, I've been saying no to a few things. Like the gallery openings downtown, or those lovely birdwatching trips I used to enjoy so much. I even had to ask my daughter if she wouldn't mind driving me when I go to visit her, which... oh, it's just so embarrassing to admit. I hate being a bother to anyone. \n\nI just... I'd rather miss out than put myself through that silly feeling on the bridge if I have to drive it myself. I hope you don't think less of me for that, Doctor. It's just a little phase, I'm sure. Am I making a bigger deal out of this than I should be?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety or worry about things other than driving on bridges in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all. I'm really quite a cheerful person, usually. I don't sit around worrying about things, goodness no. I have a lovely garden to tend to, and my books... I'm perfectly content. \n\nI've always believed in looking on the bright side, you know? There's no sense in borrowing trouble. It really is just this... this one silly little bridge thing that's been bothering me. The rest of my life is very peaceful, I assure you. I'm not a nervous sort of person in general. I hope that helps clarify things? I wouldn't want you to think I'm just a bundle of nerves about everything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this anxiety when driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I suppose it's been about 11 months now. Yes, right around then. \n\nYou see, my daughter developed a bit of a heart condition—nothing she can't handle, she's very strong—but it does mean she needs to see a specialist down in Miami quite regularly. And, well, that drive involves some rather large bridges, as I'm sure you know. \n\nBefore that, I didn't have much reason to go that way, so it really wasn't an issue. I hope it doesn't sound like I'm making excuses... I know I should be able to just drive her without making a fuss. It's just that the trips started becoming a bit frequent, and that's when I noticed this... this little hesitation. I'm sure it's just a temporary thing, don't you think? I really do try my best to be helpful to her.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that bothers you, or is it something else about them?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's not so much the height, exactly... though they are quite tall, aren't they? It's more... well, it's the water. All that open water just stretching out everywhere. \n\nI mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and goodness knows plenty of people cross them every single day without a second thought. My daughter certainly does. And I know it's perfectly safe, logically speaking. I really do. But when I just think about driving over all that water... well, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud. I know I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, and I really don't want you to think I'm losing my wits or anything. It's just a strange little quirk, I suppose. Does that make any sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it properly.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to imagine yourself driving on a bridge without feeling any anxiety?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I certainly try to. I really do. Sometimes I sit quietly in my garden and close my eyes, and I tell myself, 'It's just a road, just a simple road.' And I want so badly to just picture myself driving across it with a smile. \n\nBut... well, the moment I try to imagine actually being up there, with all that water around, my chest does this silly little flutter. I suppose I haven't quite been able to picture it being completely calm, as much as I wish I could. I know I should be able to—a woman my age ought to have more sense than this. I'm trying very hard to be positive about it, though. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm just being difficult or stubborn? I really do want to get past this so I can be more help to my daughter. I just haven't quite gotten the hang of imagining it yet, I suppose. Is it alright that I'm having a little trouble with that part?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this anxiety when you're a passenger in a car driving on a bridge, or is it only when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly when I'm the one driving, but... well, to be perfectly honest, I've started avoiding them altogether, even as a passenger. \n\nI feel so foolish admitting this, but I've actually started turning down invitations to things I used to love. Little gallery openings, my birdwatching group trips... even some visits with my daughter, unless someone else can drive me over those bridges. And even then, I just... I'd rather not go if I can help it. \n\nIt's terribly embarrassing, really. I've always been the one who planned everything and handled things myself, you know? I've always prided myself on being independent. And now I'm making a fuss and missing out on things just because of a silly road. I hope you don't think less of me for it... I know it's not very becoming of someone my age to act this way. I really am trying to be better about it, I promise. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are on a bridge but not in a car, such as when walking or biking?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, walking or biking? Well, I... I haven't ridden a bicycle in years, I'm afraid. And as for walking... well, I really couldn't say. \n\nYou see, the bridges around here are mostly for the highway, so there aren't really pedestrian paths on them anyway. I haven't actually had the occasion to try walking across one. I suppose if I had to, I'd try my very best to be brave about it! But since it hasn't come up, I just... I don't really know how I'd feel. \n\nI hope that's an alright answer? I'm sorry I can't be more helpful with that one. I always try to give you the best information I can, Doctor. I hope I'm doing okay?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if just thinking about being on a bridge, without actually being on one or driving, makes you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I don't think so. Just thinking about them doesn't really bother me. I mean, I know they're just structures, just concrete and steel, after all. \n\nWhen I'm sitting in my living room, having my tea, I can think about a bridge perfectly fine. It's really only when I'm faced with the prospect of actually getting in a car and going over one that things get a bit... uncomfortable. But just the thought of them? No, that's perfectly alright. \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm trying to be as clear as possible, I really am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... nothing quite so dramatic as all that! I mean, when I'm right there at the edge of a bridge, maybe my heart flutters just a tiny bit, but... but certainly not sweating or losing my breath or anything like that. \n\nI wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart! I've always been quite healthy and put-together, you know. So, no, I haven't had anything like that sudden, intense feeling you're describing. I just get a little... flustered about the driving part, that's all. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I really do try to keep a level head about me, mostly. I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information... am I doing alright with these answers, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if someone else was driving you across the bridge?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... if someone else is driving? I suppose... well, yes, I still feel a bit anxious, but I can manage it if I absolutely must. I'd much rather avoid it, though. \n\nActually, I... well, I haven't been going out quite as much lately because of it. I've started turning down invitations to gallery openings, and... and my birdwatching trips, which I usually adore. But if my daughter is driving, I can usually just close my eyes and take a deep breath, and we get through it. It's not pleasant, but I can do it. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I know I must sound awfully fussy, making such distinctions. I really do try not to make a fuss when others are driving me. I just sit quietly until we're across. Is that... is that a helpful way to explain it, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or uncomfortable in other high places, like tall buildings or balconies?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all! Tall buildings and balconies are perfectly fine. I actually have a lovely little balcony at my apartment, on the fourth floor, where I keep my potted geraniums. I go out there every morning to water them and enjoy the breeze. \n\nAnd, oh, I used to love going to the top of those observation decks in the city when my husband was alive... the views were just magnificent. So, no, heights themselves don't bother me in the slightest. \n\nIt really is just... just the bridges, and specifically when I'm driving over them. I know it seems a bit particular, doesn't it? I do try to be as straightforward as I can... is this the sort of detail you need, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are on a boat or a ferry, or is your anxiety limited to driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Being out on the water is quite lovely, really. \n\nSo, yes, it truly is just limited to the bridges. Oh, well... I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I've never actually been in one of those self-driving cars. They seem a bit... well, a bit futuristic for someone my age, I suppose!

I hope it doesn't sound like I'm making excuses... I know I should be able to just drive her without making a fuss. It's just that the trips started becoming a bit frequent, and that's when I noticed this... this little hesitation. I'm sure it's just a temporary thing, don't you think? I really do try my best to be helpful to her.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that bothers you, or is it something else about them?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's not so much the height, exactly... though they are quite tall, aren't they? It's more... well, it's the water. All that open water just stretching out everywhere. \n\nI mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and goodness knows plenty of people cross them every single day without a second thought. My daughter certainly does. And I know it's perfectly safe, logically speaking. I really do. But when I just think about driving over all that water... well, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud. I know I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, and I really don't want you to think I'm losing my wits or anything. It's just a strange little quirk, I suppose. Does that make any sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it properly.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to imagine yourself driving on a bridge without feeling any anxiety?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I certainly try to. I really do. Sometimes I sit quietly in my garden and close my eyes, and I tell myself, 'It's just a road, just a simple road.' And I want so badly to just picture myself driving across it with a smile. \n\nBut... well, the moment I try to imagine actually being up there, with all that water around, my chest does this silly little flutter. I suppose I haven't quite been able to picture it being completely calm, as much as I wish I could. I know I should be able to—a woman my age ought to have more sense than this. I'm trying very hard to be positive about it, though. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm just being difficult or stubborn? I really do want to get past this so I can be more help to my daughter. I just haven't quite gotten the hang of imagining it yet, I suppose. Is it alright that I'm having a little trouble with that part?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this anxiety when you're a passenger in a car driving on a bridge, or is it only when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly when I'm the one driving, but... well, to be perfectly honest, I've started avoiding them altogether, even as a passenger. \n\nI feel so foolish admitting this, but I've actually started turning down invitations to things I used to love. Little gallery openings, my birdwatching group trips... even some visits with my daughter, unless someone else can drive me over those bridges. And even then, I just... I'd rather not go if I can help it. \n\nIt's terribly embarrassing, really. I've always been the one who planned everything and handled things myself, you know? I've always prided myself on being independent. And now I'm making a fuss and missing out on things just because of a silly road. I hope you don't think less of me for it... I know it's not very becoming of someone my age to act this way. I really am trying to be better about it, I promise. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are on a bridge but not in a car, such as when walking or biking?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, walking or biking? Well, I... I haven't ridden a bicycle in years, I'm afraid. And as for walking... well, I really couldn't say. \n\nYou see, the bridges around here are mostly for the highway, so there aren't really pedestrian paths on them anyway. I haven't actually had the occasion to try walking across one. I suppose if I had to, I'd try my very best to be brave about it! But since it hasn't come up, I just... I don't really know how I'd feel. \n\nI hope that's an alright answer? I'm sorry I can't be more helpful with that one. I always try to give you the best information I can, Doctor. I hope I'm doing okay?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if just thinking about being on a bridge, without actually being on one or driving, makes you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I don't think so. Just thinking about them doesn't really bother me. I mean, I know they're just structures, just concrete and steel, after all. \n\nWhen I'm sitting in my living room, having my tea, I can think about a bridge perfectly fine. It's really only when I'm faced with the prospect of actually getting in a car and going over one that things get a bit... uncomfortable. But just the thought of them? No, that's perfectly alright. \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm trying to be as clear as possible, I really am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... nothing quite so dramatic as all that! I mean, when I'm right there at the edge of a bridge, maybe my heart flutters just a tiny bit, but... but certainly not sweating or losing my breath or anything like that. \n\nI wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart! I've always been quite healthy and put-together, you know. So, no, I haven't had anything like that sudden, intense feeling you're describing. I just get a little... flustered about the driving part, that's all. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I really do try to keep a level head about me, mostly. I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information... am I doing alright with these answers, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if someone else was driving you across the bridge?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... if someone else is driving? I suppose... well, yes, I still feel a bit anxious, but I can manage it if I absolutely must. I'd much rather avoid it, though. \n\nActually, I... well, I haven't been going out quite as much lately because of it. I've started turning down invitations to gallery openings, and... and my birdwatching trips, which I usually adore. I've even missed a few visits with my daughter recently, unless she or someone else can come and drive me over those bridges. \n\nIt's... well, it's terribly embarrassing to admit, if I'm being honest. I've always been the one who planned everything, the one who handled things myself. I've always been so independent, you see. To have to rely on others just to get across a bridge... it makes me feel a bit silly. \n\nI do hope you don't think less of me for it. I really am trying my best to handle it gracefully. Is it... is it normal to feel so embarrassed about something like this, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are on a bridge in a car with someone else driving, or is your anxiety only present when you are the one driving?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. If someone else is driving, I do still feel a little... unsettled, I guess you could say. But it's certainly much more manageable than if I'm the one behind the wheel. \n\nWhen I'm driving, it's just... well, it feels like all the responsibility is on me, and that makes it quite a bit harder. But if my daughter is driving, I can usually just close my eyes and take a deep breath, and we get through it. It's not pleasant, but I can do it. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I know I must sound awfully fussy, making such distinctions. I really do try not to make a fuss when others are driving me. I just sit quietly until we're across. Is that... is that a helpful way to explain it, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or uncomfortable in other high places, like tall buildings or balconies?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all! Tall buildings and balconies are perfectly fine. I actually have a lovely little balcony at my apartment, on the fourth floor, where I keep my potted geraniums. I go out there every morning to water them and enjoy the breeze. \n\nAnd, oh, I used to love going to the top of those observation decks in the city when my husband was alive... the views were just magnificent. So, no, heights themselves don't bother me in the slightest. \n\nIt really is just... just the bridges, and specifically when I'm driving over them. I know it seems a bit particular, doesn't it? I hope I'm not making things too complicated for you to figure out. I do try to be as straightforward as I can... is this the sort of detail you need, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are on a boat or a ferry, or is your anxiety limited to driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Boats and ferries don't bother me in the slightest. Actually, I... well, my late husband and I used to take the ferry across the bay quite often, and I always found it rather peaceful. Being out on the water is quite lovely, really. \n\nSo, yes, it truly is just limited to the bridges. I know it must seem terribly odd that one very specific thing bothers me so much while everything else is perfectly fine... I do hope I'm not being too confusing or difficult to understand. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right sort of information. Am I answering your questions properly, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about bridges change if you were driving an electric or self-driving car across the bridge?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I've never actually been in one of those self-driving cars. They seem a bit... well, a bit futuristic for someone my age, I suppose! \n\nBut, um, thinking about it... I don't believe it would make much of a difference. It's really the... the being on the bridge itself, feeling like I'm the one responsible for getting us across safely. Even if the car was doing the driving, I think I'd still feel quite on edge, just knowing we're up there. \n\nI suppose it's silly to worry about things like that when technology is so advanced now. I hope I don't sound too old-fashioned or foolish for saying that. Does that make sense, Doctor? Well, it's... it's a bit better if someone else is driving, especially if it's my daughter or a friend I trust. I don't feel quite as panicky as I do when I'm behind the wheel. But, um... it still makes me terribly uncomfortable. 

Actually, I... I suppose I should just say it. I've been trying to avoid the bridges altogether lately. I know it sounds silly, but I've started turning down invitations to things I really enjoy... gallery openings, my birdwatching group trips, and even visits to my daughter's house. I just... It really embarrasses me to admit that, Doctor. I've always been the sort of person who planned everything and handled things myself, you know? Being so dependent on others just to cross a bridge... it feels a bit pathetic. I hope you don't think less of me for letting this get so out of hand. I'm trying my best to manage it, really I am. Is... is it normal for people to avoid things like this when they get anxious?

I'm perfectly content. \n\nI've always believed in looking on the bright side, you know? There's no sense in borrowing trouble. I really do try my best to be helpful to her.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that bothers you, or is it something else about them?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's not so much the height, exactly... though they are quite tall, aren't they? It's more... well, it's the water. All that open water just stretching out everywhere. \n\nI mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and goodness knows plenty of people cross them every single day without a second thought. My daughter certainly does. And I know it's perfectly safe, logically speaking. I really do. But when I just think about driving over all that water... well, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud. I know I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, and I really don't want you to think I'm losing my wits or anything. It's just a strange little quirk, I suppose. Does that make any sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it properly.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to imagine yourself driving on a bridge without feeling any anxiety?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I certainly try to. I really do. Sometimes I sit quietly in my garden and close my eyes, and I tell myself, 'It's just a road, just a simple road.' And I want so badly to just picture myself driving across it with a smile. \n\nBut... well, the moment I try to imagine actually being up there, with all that water around, my chest does this silly little flutter. I suppose I haven't quite been able to picture it being completely calm, as much as I wish I could. I know I should be able to—a woman my age ought to have more sense than this. I'm trying very hard to be positive about it, though. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm just being difficult or stubborn? I really do want to get past this so I can be more help to my daughter. I just haven't quite gotten the hang of imagining it yet, I suppose. Is it alright that I'm having a little trouble with that part?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this anxiety when you're a passenger in a car driving on a bridge, or is it only when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly when I'm the one driving, but... well, to be perfectly honest, I've started avoiding them altogether, even as a passenger. \n\nI feel so foolish admitting this, but I've actually started turning down invitations to things I used to love. Little gallery openings, my birdwatching group trips... even some visits with my daughter, unless someone else can drive me over those bridges. And even then, I just... I'd rather not go if I can help it. \n\nIt's terribly embarrassing, really. I've always been the one who planned everything and handled things myself, you know? I've always prided myself on being independent. And now I'm making a fuss and missing out on things just because of a silly road. I hope you don't think less of me for it... I know it's not very becoming of someone my age to act this way. I really am trying to be better about it, I promise. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are on a bridge but not in a car, such as when walking or biking?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, walking or biking? Well, I... I haven't ridden a bicycle in years, I'm afraid. And as for walking... well, I really couldn't say. \n\nYou see, the bridges around here are mostly for the highway, so there aren't really pedestrian paths on them anyway. I haven't actually had the occasion to try walking across one. I suppose if I had to, I'd try my very best to be brave about it! But since it hasn't come up, I just... I don't really know how I'd feel. \n\nI hope that's an alright answer? I'm sorry I can't be more helpful with that one. I always try to give you the best information I can, Doctor. I hope I'm doing okay?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if just thinking about being on a bridge, without actually being on one or driving, makes you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I don't think so. Just thinking about them doesn't really bother me. I mean, I know they're just structures, just concrete and steel, after all. \n\nWhen I'm sitting in my living room, having my tea, I can think about a bridge perfectly fine. It's really only when I'm faced with the prospect of actually getting in a car and going over one that things get a bit... uncomfortable. But just the thought of them? No, that's perfectly alright. \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm trying to be as clear as possible, I really am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... nothing quite so dramatic as all that! I mean, when I'm right there at the edge of a bridge, maybe my heart flutters just a tiny bit, but... but certainly not sweating or losing my breath or anything like that. \n\nI wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart! I've always been quite healthy and put-together, you know. So, no, I haven't had anything like that sudden, intense feeling you're describing. I just get a little... flustered about the driving part, that's all. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I really do try to keep a level head about me, mostly. I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information... am I doing alright with these answers, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if someone else was driving you across the bridge?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... if someone else is driving? I suppose... well, yes, I still feel a bit anxious, but I can manage it if I absolutely must. I'd much rather avoid it, though. \n\nActually, I... well, I haven't been going out quite as much lately because of it. I've started turning down invitations to gallery openings, and... and my birdwatching trips, which I usually adore. I've always been the one who planned everything, the one who handled things myself. I've always been so independent, you see. To have to rely on others just to get across a bridge... it makes me feel a bit silly. \n\nI do hope you don't think less of me for it. I really am trying my best to handle it gracefully. Is it... is it normal to feel so embarrassed about something like this, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are on a bridge in a car with someone else driving, or is your anxiety only present when you are the one driving?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. If someone else is driving, I do still feel a little... unsettled, I guess you could say. I know I must sound awfully fussy, making such distinctions. I really do try not to make a fuss when others are driving me. I just sit quietly until we're across. Is that... is that a helpful way to explain it, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or uncomfortable in other high places, like tall buildings or balconies?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all!
